Known Results

Before presenting our new results, we first recall some previously established findings. We summarize key results concerning special trees as well as known imbalance indices.

Proposition 2.4

(Coronado et al. (2020), Theorem 1 and Proposition 6) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}n∈N≥1. The mb-tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{mb}_n$$\end{document}Tnmb and the gfb-tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{gfb}_n$$\end{document}Tngfb minimize the Colless index on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}BTn∗.

Remark 2.5

Notice that for most leaf numbers n, there are trees distinct from the mb-tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{mb}_n$$\end{document}Tnmb and the gfb-tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{gfb}_n$$\end{document}Tngfb that also minimize the Colless index. However, all binary trees with n leaves minimizing the Colless index have been completely characterized by Coronado et al. (2020), Proposition 1 and Proposition 3.

Lemma 2.6

(Fischer (2021), Theorem 2) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal {T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}T∈Tn∗ with . Then, T minimizes the Sackin index on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}BTn∗ if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T = T^{fb}_{h_n}$$\end{document}T=Tnfb or T employs precisely two leaf depths, namely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_n-1$$\end{document}hn-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_n$$\end{document}hn. Moreover, in this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S(T) = -2^{h_n} + n \cdot (h_n+1)$$\end{document}S(T)=-2hn+n·(hn+1), which equals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_n \cdot 2^{h_n}$$\end{document}hn·2hn if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n = 2^{h_n}$$\end{document}n=2hn.

Remark 2.7

Note that trees with n leaves minimizing the Sackin index as characterized in the lemma above are precisely those trees that can be constructed from the fb-tree of height \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_n-1$$\end{document}hn-1 by attaching \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n - 2^{h_n-1}$$\end{document}n-2hn-1 cherries to its leaves. In particular, the gfb-tree and the mb-tree can be constructed in this way. This follows from the fact that the gfb-tree and the mb-tree minimize the Colless index on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}BTn∗ (Proposition 2.4) and the fact that all trees minimizing the Colless index also minimize the Sackin index on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}BTn∗ (Coronado et al. (2020), Proposition 9). Note that to construct the gfb-tree, one has to attach the cherries to the fb-tree from left to right (or vice versa) (Cleary et al. (2025), Lemma 4.17).

Proposition 2.8

(Coronado et al. (2020), Theorem 3) For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}_{\ge 1}$$\end{document}n∈N≥1, let . We are now in the position to state our new results.

Results

This section is divided into two subsections. The first subsection examines the underlying tree shape sequences of the metaconcepts, highlighting their differences and similarities. The second subsection analyzes each metaconcept in terms of its minimizing and maximizing trees, as well as its minimum and maximum values. Finally, we investigate their locality and recursiveness.

Tree Shape Sequences

In this subsection, we analyze and compare three sequences derived from a (binary) tree: the balance value sequence, the clade size sequence, and the leaf depth sequence. Each of these sequences serves as the foundation for a specific metaconcept.

A shared property of the three sequences associated with a rooted tree is that they can be computed recursively. We will exploit this property to analyze the recursiveness of our metaconcepts.

To formalize the recursive structure of these sequences, we introduce an operator that allows us to merge two sequences while preserving ascending order. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_1$$\end{document}Seq1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_2$$\end{document}Seq2 be two sequences of lengths \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1$$\end{document}n1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_2$$\end{document}n2, respectively. We define their ordered union as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_1 \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ Seq_2 :=Seq$$\end{document}Seq1∪→Seq2:=Seq, where Seq is a sequence of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1 + n_2$$\end{document}n1+n2 containing all elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_1$$\end{document}Seq1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_2$$\end{document}Seq2 arranged in ascending order. For example, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1,4,5,13) \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ (2,2,4,7,8) = (1,2,2,4,4,5,7,8,13)$$\end{document}(1,4,5,13)∪→(2,2,4,7,8)=(1,2,2,4,4,5,7,8,13). Additionally, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}a∈N, we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_1 + a$$\end{document}Seq1+a as the sequence obtained by increasing each element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq_1$$\end{document}Seq1 by a. For example, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1,4,5,13) +1 = (2,5,6,14)$$\end{document}(1,4,5,13)+1=(2,5,6,14).

Remark 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}T∈BTn∗ be a binary tree with standard decomposition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T = \left( T_1, T_2\right) $$\end{document}T=T1,T2 such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_1$$\end{document}T1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_2$$\end{document}T2 have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1$$\end{document}n1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_2$$\end{document}n2 leaves, respectively. Notice that all inner vertices of a maximal pending subtree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti have the same balance value in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti as in T and the root of T has balance value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| n_1 - n_2\right| $$\end{document}n1-n2. Hence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {B}\left( T\right) = \mathcal {B}(T_1) \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ \mathcal {B}(T_2) \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ (|n_1 - n_2|). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}BT=B(T1)∪→B(T2)∪→(|n1-n2|).Now, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal {T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}T∈Tn∗ be an arbitrary tree with standard decomposition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T = (T_1, \ldots , T_k)$$\end{document}T=(T1,…,Tk) such that the maximal pending subtree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_i$$\end{document}ni leaves. Note that all inner vertices of a maximal pending subtree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti have the same clade size in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti as in T and the root of T has clade size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1 + \ldots + n_k = n$$\end{document}n1+…+nk=n. Also note that the depth of a leaf in a maximal pending subtree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti is one less than in T. Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {N}(T) = \mathcal {N}(T_1) \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ \ldots \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ \mathcal {N}(T_k) \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ \underbrace{(n_1 + \ldots + n_k)}_{=n} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N(T)=N(T1)∪→…∪→N(Tk)∪→(n1+…+nk)⏟=nand\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Delta (T) = \left( \Delta (T_1) \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ \ldots \overset{\rightarrow }{\cup }\ \Delta (T_k)\right) +1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Δ(T)=Δ(T1)∪→…∪→Δ(Tk)+1.

Another shared property of these sequences is that none of them uniquely characterize a (binary) tree. That is, two non-isomorphic (binary) trees can have the same sequence (see Figures 10 and 11 in Appendix A). Moreover, examples exist where two distinct binary trees with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 4$$\end{document}n≥4 leaves have the same/different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}$$\end{document}B and/or the same/different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N and/or the same/different \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta $$\end{document}Δ. A (unique) minimal example in terms of the leaf number n for each possible pair of sequences is given in Figures 10, 11, 12, 13 and 14 in Appendix A. Note that all figures except for Figure 14 show a unique minimal example

Metaconcepts

In this section, we analyze the three previously introduced metaconcepts. Specifically, we determine which families of the function f ensure that a given metaconcept yields a (binary) imbalance index. Accordingly, we examine the trees that minimize and maximize each metaconcept based on the choice of f and provide formulas for computing their minimum and maximum values. Finally, we analyze the locality and the recursiveness of the metaconcepts.

Throughout this manuscript, we focus exclusively on first-order metaconcepts. However, all results regarding minimizing and maximizing trees extend to higher-order metaconcepts that are equivalent to the first-order case. For examples of such functions, see the following remark.

Remark 3.2

The BVM, the LDM, and the binary CSM of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \ge 2$$\end{document}ω≥2 with a function of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(x,o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1}) = f_1(x) \cdot f_2(o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1}) + f_3(o_{1}, \ldots , o_{\omega -1})$$\end{document}f(x,o1,…,oω-1)=f1(x)·f2(o1,…,oω-1)+f3(o1,…,oω-1) are equivalent to the first-order metaconcept with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(x) = f_1(x)$$\end{document}f(x)=f1(x), provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2(o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1}) > 0$$\end{document}f2(o1,…,oω-1)>0 and the additional values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1}$$\end{document}o1,…,oω-1 are the same for all trees with the same number of leaves (e.g., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_i = n$$\end{document}oi=n but \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$o_i \ne h(T)$$\end{document}oi≠h(T)). This equivalence holds because the additional values act as constants, and the number of summands in the calculation of these metaconcepts remains the same for all trees with n leaves.

For arbitrary trees, the number of summands in the CSM varies. Thus, the CSM of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega \ge 2$$\end{document}ω≥2 can only be guaranteed to be equivalent to the first-order metaconcept if the function is of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(x,o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1}) = f_1(x) \cdot f_2(o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1})$$\end{document}f(x,o1,…,oω-1)=f1(x)·f2(o1,…,oω-1), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2(o_1, \ldots , o_{\omega -1}) > 0$$\end{document}f2(o1,…,oω-1)>0. In this case, the metaconcept remains equivalent to the first-order metaconcept with function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(x) = f_1(x)$$\end{document}f(x)=f1(x).

Conversely, a cross (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×) indicates that for at least one function in the given family, the metaconcept fails to be a (binary) imbalance index. The entry “depends” means that the binary minimizing tree(s) are not the same for all functions within that family. An entry in square brackets indicates that this result is adapted from Cleary et al. This table provides an overview of our results, indicating for which families of the function f the metaconcepts qualify as (binary) imbalance indices. The column labels refer to four cases, all of which require f to be strictly increasing. A checkmark (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\checkmark $$\end{document}✓) indicates that no further conditions on f are needed to satisfy the corresponding property. When additional constraints on f are required, they are explicitly stated in the respective cell. Conversely, a cross (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×) indicates that for at least one function in the given family, the metaconcept fails to be a (binary) imbalance index. The entry “depends” means that the binary minimizing tree(s) are not the same for all functions within that family. An entry in square brackets indicates that this result is adapted from Cleary et al. (2025), Corollary 4.4

Remark 3.3

In this remark, we provide examples to illustrate some of the cases presented in Table 4.

First, we provide an example showing that the minimizing tree of the BVM can vary for strictly increasing (and possibly strictly concave) functions f. To cover both cases, consider two strictly increasing and strictly concave functions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1$$\end{document}f1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2$$\end{document}f2, defined as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1(x) = \log _2\left( \frac{1}{2}x +1\right) $$\end{document}f1(x)=log212x+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2(x) = \log _2\left( \frac{3}{2}x +1\right) $$\end{document}f2(x)=log232x+1.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n = 5$$\end{document}n=5, where three binary trees exist: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{gfb}_5$$\end{document}T5gfb, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_1 = \left( T^{fb}_2,T^{fb}_0\right) $$\end{document}T1=T2fb,T0fb, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{cat}_5$$\end{document}T5cat. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \{1,2\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,2}, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_i}\left( T^{gfb}_5\right)&= 2 \cdot f_i(0) + 2 \cdot f_i(1), \\ \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_i}(T_1)&= 3 \cdot f_i(0) + f_i(3), \\ \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_i}\left( T^{cat}_5\right)&= f_i(0) + f_i(1) + f_i(2) + f_i(3) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΦiBT5gfb=2·fi(0)+2·fi(1),ΦiB(T1)=3·fi(0)+fi(3),ΦiBT5cat=fi(0)+fi(1)+fi(2)+fi(3)and hence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_1}\left( T^{gfb}_5\right)&\approx 1.17, \quad \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_2}\left( T^{gfb}_5\right) \approx 2.64, \\ \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_1}(T_1)&\approx 1.32, \quad \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_2}(T_1) \approx 2.46, \\ \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_1}\left( T^{cat}_5\right)&\approx 2.91, \quad \Phi ^{\mathcal {B}}_{f_2}\left( T^{cat}_5\right) \approx 5.78. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ1BT5gfb≈1.17,Φ2BT5gfb≈2.64,Φ1B(T1)≈1.32,Φ2B(T1)≈2.46,Φ1BT5cat≈2.91,Φ2BT5cat≈5.78.Thus, for the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \begin{document}$$f_1$$\end{document}f1, the gfb-tree is the unique minimizer, while for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} Second, we demonstrate that the CSM is not a (binary) imbalance index for all strictly increasing functions f. Specifically, we show that the fb-tree is not the unique minimizing tree on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}BTn∗. Consider the tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_2 = \left( T^{gfb}_5, T^{gfb}_3\right) $$\end{document}T2=T5gfb,T3gfb, for which the clade size sequence is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}(T_2) = (2,2,2,3,3,5,8).$$\end{document}N(T2)=(2,2,2,3,3,5,8).Similarly, for the fully balanced tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{fb}_3$$\end{document}T3fb, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}\left( T^{fb}_3\right) = (2,2,2,2,4,4,8).$$\end{document}NT3fb=(2,2,2,2,4,4,8).Now, define the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_3$$\end{document}f3 as follows:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_3(x) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} x & \text {if } x \le 3, \\ x+2 & \text {if } x > 3. \end{array}\right. }$$\end{document}f3(x)=xifx≤3,x+2ifx>3.With this function, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\mathcal {N}}_{f_3}(T_2) = 29 < 30 = \Phi ^{\mathcal {N}}_{f_3}\left( T^{fb}_3\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ3N(T2)=29<30=Φ3NT3fb.Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_2$$\end{document}T2 attains a smaller value than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{fb}_3$$\end{document}T3fb, proving that the fb-tree is not always a (unique) minimizer. Consequently, the CSM is not a (binary) imbalance index for all strictly increasing functions f.

Third, we show that the LDM is not a (binary) imbalance index for all strictly increasing (and possibly strictly concave) functions f. Again, to cover both cases, we show that the fb-tree is not the unique tree minimizing the LDM on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{B}\mathcal{T}^{*}_n$$\end{document}BTn∗ for a chosen strictly increasing and strictly concave function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_4$$\end{document}f4, defined as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_4(x) = \frac{x}{x+\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}f4(x)=xx+12. This function is strictly increasing. Moreover, it is strictly concave, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_4'(x) = \frac{1}{2\left( x^2+x+\frac{1}{4}\right) }$$\end{document}f4′(x)=12x2+x+14 is strictly decreasing for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in \mathbb {R}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}x∈R≥0. Then, we have for the fully balanced tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{fb}_2$$\end{document}T2fb,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\Delta }_{f_4}\left( T^{fb}_2\right) = 4 \cdot f_4(2) = 3.2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ4ΔT2fb=4·f4(2)=3.2.For the caterpillar tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{cat}_4$$\end{document}T4cat, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\Delta }_{f_4}\left( T^{cat}_4\right) = f_4(1) + f_4(2) + 2 \cdot f_4(3) \approx 3.18 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ4ΔT4cat=f4(1)+f4(2)+2·f4(3)≈3.18Thus,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\Delta }_{f_4}\left( T^{fb}_2\right) > \Phi ^{\Delta }_{f_4}\left( T^{cat}_4\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ4ΔT2fb>Φ4ΔT4cat,showing that the LDM is not a (binary) imbalance index for all strictly increasing (and possibly strictly concave) functions f.

Fourth, we show that the minimizing tree for the LDM with a strictly increasing and strictly concave function f also depends on the choice of f. In the previous calculation, we observed that the caterpillar minimizes the LDM for the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_4$$\end{document}f4 defined above. Now, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_5 = \log _2$$\end{document}f5=log2 be another strictly increasing and strictly concave function. Then, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{\Delta }_{f_5}\left( T^{fb}_2\right) = 4 < 4.17 \approx \Phi ^{\Delta }_{f_5}\left( T^{cat}_4\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ5ΔT2fb=4<4.17≈Φ5ΔT4cat.Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^{fb}_2$$\end{document}T2fb attains the minimum for the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_5$$\end{document}f5, illustrating that the minimizing tree for the LDM depends on the choice of f.

Choosing a suitable (binary) imbalance index derived from a metaconcept

Before measuring tree balance, three key questions must be addressed: Are the trees binary or arbitrary? Which binary tree(s) should be considered the most balanced? Which aspect of the tree (balance values, clade sizes, or leaf depths) should be used to measure balance? Once these questions are answered, the next step is to determine which imbalance index, derived from which metaconcept, is most suitable.

To support this decision, we provide four decision trees in Figures 3 and 4. Each figure contains two decision trees: one for binary trees and one for arbitrary trees. The decision trees in Figure 3 begin with a choice of binary minimizing tree(s), while those in Figure 4 start with the aspect of the tree to be considered, i.e., the class of metaconcepts, and then proceed to the selection of binary minimizing trees.

In both figures, the notations “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\equiv C(T)$$\end{document}≡C(T)” and “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\equiv S(T)$$\end{document}≡S(T)” indicate that the resulting imbalance index is equivalent to the Colless or Sackin index, respectively. The label “\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop {\mathrm {arg\,min}}\limits S(T)$$\end{document}argminS(T)” indicates that the binary minimizing trees coincide with those of the Sackin index, although the imbalance index itself may not be equivalent to the Sackin index (as it can lead to different rankings of non-extremal trees).

Calculating the metaconcepts in R

Here, we provide R code to calculate the three metaconcepts using the R packages ape Paradis and Schliep (2019) and treebalance Fischer et al. (2023).

General result regarding minimizing trees and sequences

To investigate the extremal trees associated with these metaconcepts, we frequently use the following lemma. Note that the first part of this lemma is a generalization of (Fischer and Liebscher (2021), Theorem 2).

Lemma 3.4

Conversely, if a tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T satisfies for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T~∈T and all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} $$\end{document}i∈1,…,l\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i \le Seq(\widetilde{T})_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≤Seq(T~)i, then T minimizes the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f$$\end{document}ΦfSeq on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T for all (not necessarily strictly) increasing functions.

Conversely, if a tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T satisfies for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T~∈T and all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} $$\end{document}i∈1,…,l\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i \le Seq(\widetilde{T})_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≤Seq(T~)i, then T minimizes the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f$$\end{document}ΦfSeq on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T for all (not necessarily strictly) increasing functions.

If T uniquely minimizes the functional for some increasing function f, then we have for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \left\{ T\right\} $$\end{document}T~∈T\T\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i < Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for at least one } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i<Seq(T~)ifor at least onei∈1,…,l. Conversely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i \le Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for all } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≤Seq(T~)ifor alli∈1,…,l and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i < Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for at least one } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i<Seq(T~)ifor at least onei∈1,…,l for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \left\{ T\right\} $$\end{document}T~∈T\T, then T (uniquely) minimizes the functional for all (strictly) increasing functions f.

If T uniquely minimizes the functional for some increasing function f, then we have for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \left\{ T\right\} $$\end{document}T~∈T\T\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i < Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for at least one } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i<Seq(T~)ifor at least onei∈1,…,l. Conversely, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i \le Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for all } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≤Seq(T~)ifor alli∈1,…,l and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i < Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for at least one } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i<Seq(T~)ifor at least onei∈1,…,l for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \left\{ T\right\} $$\end{document}T~∈T\T, then T (uniquely) minimizes the functional for all (strictly) increasing functions f.

Proof

We prove this assertion by contradiction. Let T minimize the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f$$\end{document}ΦfSeq for all strictly increasing functions f. Assume that there exists a tree \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T~∈T such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(T)_i > Seq(\widetilde{T})_i$$\end{document}Seq(T)i>Seq(T~)i for at least one \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \left\{ 1, \ldots , l\right\} $$\end{document}i∈1,…,l. For the rest of the proof let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i_{min}$$\end{document}imin be the smallest i with this property. The strategy of the proof is now to construct a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{f}$$\end{document}f~ that is strictly increasing and yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_{\widetilde{f}}(T) > \Phi ^{Seq}_{\widetilde{f}}(\widetilde{T})$$\end{document}Φf~Seq(T)>Φf~Seq(T~), leading to a contradiction.Note that the sequence Seq is sorted in ascending order. Thus, T has more entries in its sequence Seq(T) whose value is at least \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(T)_{i_{min}}$$\end{document}Seq(T)imin than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T}$$\end{document}T~ has in its sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(\widetilde{T})$$\end{document}Seq(T~). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{T}$$\end{document}mT be the number of entries in Seq(T) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(T)_j \ge Seq(T)_{i_{min}}$$\end{document}Seq(T)j≥Seq(T)imin, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{T} = l - i_{min} + 1$$\end{document}mT=l-imin+1. Moreover, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{\widetilde{T}}$$\end{document}mT~ be the number of entries in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(\widetilde{T})$$\end{document}Seq(T~) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(\widetilde{T})_j \ge Seq(T)_{i_{min}}$$\end{document}Seq(T~)j≥Seq(T)imin. Then we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{\widetilde{T}} < m_{T}$$\end{document}mT~<mT and thus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_{\widetilde{T}} - m_{T} \le -1$$\end{document}mT~-mT≤-1.The idea of the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{f}$$\end{document}f~ now is to take the identity function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$id $$\end{document}id and add a penalty term x to values greater or equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(T)_{i_{min}}$$\end{document}Seq(T)imin. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D :=\Phi ^{Seq}_{id }(\widetilde{T}) - \Phi ^{Seq}_{id }(T)$$\end{document}D:=ΦidSeq(T~)-ΦidSeq(T) be the difference of the functional applied to T and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T}$$\end{document}T~ when using the identity function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$id $$\end{document}id, which is strictly increasing, thus implying \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D\ge 0$$\end{document}D≥0.Now, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathbb {R}_{>D}$$\end{document}x∈R>D. We then define the strictly increasing function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{f}$$\end{document}f~ as follows: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{f}(s) :={\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} s, \text { if } s < Seq(T)_{i_{min}}\\ s + x, \text { if } s \ge Seq(T)_{i_{min}}\\ \end{array}\right. }.$$\end{document}f~(s):=s,ifs<Seq(T)imins+x,ifs≥Seq(T)imin. This yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{Seq}_{\widetilde{f}}(\widetilde{T}) - \Phi ^{Seq}_{\widetilde{f}}(T)&= \left( \Phi ^{Seq}_{id }(\widetilde{T}) + m_{\widetilde{T}} \cdot x\right) - \left( \Phi ^{Seq}_{id }(T) + m_{T} \cdot x\right) \\&= D + \underbrace{\left( m_{\widetilde{T}} - m_{T}\right) }_{\le -1} \cdot \underbrace{x}_{> D} < 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φf~Seq(T~)-Φf~Seq(T)=ΦidSeq(T~)+mT~·x-ΦidSeq(T)+mT·x=D+mT~-mT⏟≤-1·x⏟>D<0. This contradicts the assumption that T minimizes the functional for all strictly increasing functions and thus completes the proof for this part.Now, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T and suppose that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T~∈T and for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,\ldots ,l\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,…,l}, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(T)_i \le Seq(\widetilde{T})_i.$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≤Seq(T~)i. For any increasing function f, it follows that applying f to each entry preserves the order, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(Seq(T)_i) \le f(Seq(\widetilde{T})_i)$$\end{document}f(Seq(T)i)≤f(Seq(T~)i) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} $$\end{document}i∈1,…,l. Summing over all indices, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{Seq}_f(T) = \sum \limits _{i=1}^l f(Seq(T)_i) \le \sum \limits _{i=1}^l f(Seq(\widetilde{T})_i) = \Phi ^{Seq}_f(\widetilde{T}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΦfSeq(T)=∑i=1lf(Seq(T)i)≤∑i=1lf(Seq(T~)i)=ΦfSeq(T~). Thus, T minimizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f$$\end{document}ΦfSeq for all increasing functions f, which completes the proof of this part.First, assume that T uniquely minimizes the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f$$\end{document}ΦfSeq for some increasing function f. We want to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Seq(T)_i < Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for at least one } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \text { and for all }\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \{T\}.$$\end{document}Seq(T)i<Seq(T~)ifor at least onei∈1,…,land for allT~∈T\{T}. Assume that this is not the case, i.e., assume there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \{T\}$$\end{document}T^∈T\{T} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i \ge Seq(\widehat{T})_i \text { for all } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≥Seq(T^)ifor alli∈1,…,l. Then, while T might minimize the functional, T cannot minimize the functional uniquely, because, by the first part of the lemma, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f(T) \ge \Phi ^{Seq}_f(\widehat{T})$$\end{document}ΦfSeq(T)≥ΦfSeq(T^). This contradicts the assumption and completes the proof for this part.For the second assertion, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in \mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T∈T and assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i \le Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for all } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i≤Seq(T~)ifor alli∈1,…,l and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Seq(T)_i < Seq(\widetilde{T})_i \text { for at least one } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Seq(T)i<Seq(T~)ifor at least onei∈1,…,l for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widetilde{T} \in \mathcal {T} \setminus \{T\}$$\end{document}T~∈T\{T}. Then, applying any (strictly) increasing function f, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f(Seq(T)_i) \le f(Seq(\widetilde{T})_i) \text { for all } i \in \left\{ 1,\ldots ,l\right\} , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f(Seq(T)i)≤f(Seq(T~)i)for alli∈1,…,l, with strict inequality for at least one index precisely if f is strictly increasing. Summing over all indices gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi ^{Seq}_f(T) = \sum \limits _{i=1}^l f(Seq(T)_i) \le \sum \limits _{i=1}^l f(Seq(\widetilde{T})_i) = \Phi ^{Seq}_f(\widetilde{T}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΦfSeq(T)=∑i=1lf(Seq(T)i)≤∑i=1lf(Seq(T~)i)=ΦfSeq(T~), which shows that T (uniquely) minimizes the functional for all (strictly) increasing functions f. Note that the minimum is unique precisely if the inequality is strict, which is the case when f is strictly increasing. This completes the proof for minimization.The proof for the respective maximization statements follows analogously by reversing all inequalities. Thus, the entire proof is complete. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We prove this assertion by contradiction. First, assume that T uniquely minimizes the functional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi ^{Seq}_f$$\end{document}ΦfSeq for some increasing function f.
